I’ll shift my weight from one foot to the other, look at the other registers, check the time on my phone, put it away, then check it again, like that somehow makes it go faster.\n\nAnd at the clinic too, in the waiting room, I can sit there but it feels like I’m kind of forcing myself to sit there. I keep thinking, “Okay, how much longer is this going to take?” and I’ll look around or keep checking whether my name is about to be called. It’s not like I usually say anything rude, but inside I’m just very impatient and wanting it to be over with already.\n\nWith talking, I think I can wait sometimes, but if I have something I want to say, it’s hard not to jump in. I’ll be kind of waiting for the opening instead of fully relaxing into the conversation.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have difficulty keeping your workspace or living space organized?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. My room and my stuff can get disorganized pretty quickly, but I think the bigger issue is when there are several things I’m supposed to do and I don’t know how to sort them out in my head.\n\nLike if I have to call the counselor, clean up my room, and send in an application, those are all normal tasks, right? But instead of just picking one and finishing it, I’ll kind of stand there or sit there thinking, “Okay, what do I do first?” And then I’ll start cleaning for a few minutes, remember the application, open that up, then think I should make the phone call before it gets too late, but then I don’t have whatever information I need ready, so I go looking for that, and somehow I’ve touched all three things and none of them are actually done.\n\nSo it’s not always just that the space is messy, although it can be. It’s more like I don’t have a good system for where to start or how to keep going in order. I bounce around and half-finish things, and then the room or the desk ends up looking worse because I’ve pulled things out for multiple tasks at once.\n\nDoctor: Are these difficulties you're experiencing affecting your relationships, work, or daily life in a significant way?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it is affecting my daily life in a real way. The biggest example is with trying to move forward toward becoming an automotive technician.\n\nI know I can handle the hands-on part. Like, when I’m actually working with tools or dealing with something physical and practical, I feel like I can understand it and stay with it better. But the stuff around it — training deadlines, applications, paperwork, keeping track of what needs to be submitted and when — that’s where I keep messing up.\n\nI’ve missed training deadlines more than once, and I’ve had to redo applications because I either didn’t finish them right, didn’t get them in on time, or somehow let them sit too long until they weren’t usable anymore. And then it’s like I’m back at the beginning again. It’s frustrating because it’s not that I don’t want to do it. I do want to. I’m 26 and I feel like I should be moving forward by now, but I keep getting stuck on the steps that are supposed to get me there.\n\nSo, yeah, I’d say it’s significant. It’s not just annoying or inconvenient — it’s actually keeping me from getting into the work I think I could do.\n\nDoctor: Do you often experience strong emotions such as anxiety, anger, or excitement?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not totally sure how to answer that. I mean, I have emotions like anyone does, obviously, but I don’t know if I’d say I have, like, unusually strong anxiety or anger or excitement in a way that stands out to me.\n\nI can get frustrated sometimes, and I can get excited about things I care about, but I don’t know that I’ve really paid attention to whether it’s stronger than other people’s. It’s kind of hard to judge from the inside, you know? Like, if something irritates me, it feels normal in the moment because it’s my reaction.\n\nSo I guess… maybe sometimes, but I’m not sure. I wouldn’t want to say yes like it’s a major thing if I haven’t really noticed it clearly.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience these difficulties with focus, restlessness, and impulsivity at home, at work, or in social situations, or are they mainly in one of these areas?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure how to separate it out, honestly. Like, I notice it most at home, because that’s where I’m trying to manage my own stuff and there isn’t really someone standing over me telling me what the next step is. So at home it shows up a lot in trying to get things done, keep track of what I was doing, or not drift from one thing to another.\n\nWork is harder for me to answer because I’m not really in a regular job right now, so I don’t have as much to compare there. I can say that with work-related things, like applications or steps I’m supposed to complete, that’s where it becomes a problem. But I don’t know if that counts as “work” exactly or more like daily life.\n\nSocial situations… I don’t know. I haven’t really thought of it that way. I’m not saying it never happens around people, but it’s not the area where I’m most aware of it. I guess I’d say it’s not just one tiny situation, but I can explain it better with home and trying-to-get-things-done stuff than with social situations.\n\nDoctor: Have you always had these difficulties with focus and restlessness, or did they start at some point in your life?\n\nPatient: I think it’s been there for a long time, honestly. Like, when I think back, it doesn’t feel like something that suddenly started as an adult or after high school or anything. Even in elementary school, I remember teachers moving my seat around a lot because I’d talk out of turn or get distracted by whoever was near me.\n\nAnd homework was a big thing too — not always because I didn’t understand it, but because I’d forget it, or leave it somewhere, or not turn it in even if I had done part of it. I also remember rushing through worksheets just to get them done, and then there’d be these careless mistakes where, looking back, I probably could’ve gotten them right if I had slowed down.\n\nAt the time I don’t think I really understood it as a “focus” problem. It was more like I was always being told to sit somewhere else, stop talking, pay attention, or check my work. So yeah, I’d say it’s been around since I was a kid, not something that just came out of nowhere recently.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulties with staying on task and getting distracted are more of a problem for you than the restlessness and fidgeting?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. I mean, I do fidget and feel restless sometimes, but the bigger issue for me is definitely getting pulled off task.\n\nLike, I’ll sit down to do something that should be pretty straightforward, like working on an application, and then it’s like every little thing can knock me off course. A car goes by outside, my phone lights up, or I’ll suddenly think of some random thing I should check, and before I even really notice what happened I’m not doing the application anymore. I’m looking something up, or I’ve opened another tab, or I’m in another room doing some unrelated thing.\n\nIt’s not always even that I decide to stop. It’s more like my attention just gets grabbed by whatever is nearby or whatever thought pops up. Then later I realize, oh, I was supposed to be doing that other thing, and it’s frustrating because I didn’t mean to drift away from it. So yeah, compared to the fidgeting, the distraction and staying on task feels like the part that causes me more problems.\n\nDoctor: Did you have any of these difficulties with focus, restlessness, or impulsivity when you were a teenager?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. I mean, the stuff I remember most clearly is actually from even before being a teenager, like elementary school, so by the time I was a teenager it didn’t feel brand new or anything.\n\nI remember teachers always moving my seat because I’d talk out of turn or get distracted by whoever was near me. And homework was a recurring issue — I’d forget it, or not have it with me, or somehow not turn it in the way I was supposed to. I also used to rush through worksheets just to get them finished, and then I’d make these dumb careless mistakes that I probably would’ve caught if I had slowed down.\n\nSo, yeah, I’d say those kinds of problems were already there when I was younger and carried into that teenage period, rather than starting later.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your restlessness and fidgeting are as bothersome to you as your difficulty with staying focused and on task?\n\nPatient: Not as much, no. The focus thing bothers me more overall, because that’s the part where I feel like I actually lose track of what I’m supposed to be doing.\n\nThe restlessness and fidgeting is definitely there, though. Like if I’m sitting with someone, especially for more than a few minutes, my leg is usually bouncing under the table, or I’m clicking a pen, or I’ll be messing with my keys in my pocket or in my hand without even realizing I’m doing it. Sometimes I only notice because the other person looks at my hand, or I hear the clicking and realize, oh, that’s me doing that.\n\nIt can be embarrassing if I catch myself doing it, and I’ll try to stop, but then a minute later I’m doing something else with my hands or my foot starts moving again. So it’s annoying, and I’m aware of it, but I’d still say the staying-focused part causes me more trouble than the fidgeting itself.\n\nDoctor: Have your difficulties with focus, restlessness, and impulsivity caused problems in your relationships with friends or family?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think it has, although maybe not always in the obvious “we had a huge fight” kind of way. It’s more like people close to me can see that I’m not moving forward the way I keep saying I want to, and that creates tension.\n\nLike, I’ve been trying to move toward becoming an automotive technician, because I actually feel pretty confident with the hands-on part. When I’m working on something physical like that, I feel like I can understand it and do it. But the steps around it — applications, training deadlines, paperwork, following through on the things that are supposed to get me there — that’s where I keep messing up.\n\nI’ve missed deadlines for training stuff, or I’ve had to redo applications because I didn’t finish them right or didn’t get them submitted when I was supposed to. And after it happens enough times, family especially starts asking, like, “Are you actually doing this?” or “Why haven’t you finished that yet?” And I get defensive, because I know it looks like I don’t care or I’m not trying, but that’s not really how it feels from the inside.\n\nSo yeah, it affects relationships because I think people get frustrated with me, and I get embarrassed and frustrated with myself. I don’t want to keep explaining why I’m still stuck in the same place when I know I could probably handle the actual work if I could just get through the steps to get there.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these difficulties with focus, restlessness, and impulsivity in multiple settings, such as at home, in social situations, and in different types of tasks?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I’d say it shows up in more than one place. It’s not just, like, if I’m sitting at home being lazy or something. The biggest example for me is probably the whole automotive technician thing, because that’s where it actually keeps costing me progress.\n\nLike, I know I can handle the hands-on work — if I’m actually working on a car or doing something physical and practical, I feel pretty capable. But the parts around it are where I keep getting stuck. Training deadlines, applications, paperwork, following the steps in order… I’ve missed deadlines and had to redo applications more than once, enough times that I’m still not really moving forward the way I should be.\n\nAnd that’s not just one type of situation, because some of that is at home trying to sit down and fill stuff out, some of it is dealing with training programs or application systems, and some of it comes up when people ask me about it and I realize I don’t have a good answer for why it’s not done yet. It feels like the same problem follows me into different tasks, especially anything that has a deadline or a bunch of steps. So yeah, it’s definitely not limited to one setting.\n\nDoctor: Did you experience difficulties with focus, restlessness, or impulsivity as a child?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I did. I mean, looking back, it was there pretty early, even in elementary school. At the time I don’t think I understood it as “focus problems” or anything like that — it was more like I was always the kid getting corrected.\n\nTeachers were constantly moving my seat around, usually because I’d be talking when I wasn’t supposed to or blurting something out instead of waiting. I remember being moved closer to the teacher, or away from certain kids, or by myself, like the seating chart was always being changed because I couldn’t just sit there and keep quiet.\n\nAnd homework was a big thing too. I’d forget to bring it home, or I’d do it and forget to turn it in, or it would be crumpled up in my backpack somewhere. Worksheets were another one — I’d rush through them just to be done, and then I’d have all these careless mistakes where I actually knew the answers but skipped a step or didn’t read the question right. So yeah, it wasn’t just something that started recently. It was showing up when I was a kid too.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any periods in your life where these difficulties with focus, restlessness, and impulsivity seemed to improve or lessen before returning?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, it’s kind of hard to separate, because I feel like some version of it has always been there, but the point where it got this disruptive was after my soccer injury. That really knocked me out of my normal routine.\n\nBefore that, I at least had a structure I was used to, even if I wasn’t perfect with it. After the injury, everything got thrown off, and that’s when it felt like the focus and follow-through problems became a much bigger deal in my day-to-day life. Since then, it hasn’t really gone back to being manageable the way it was before.\n\nSo I’d say it’s been at this level for about… maybe a year and ten months now. Almost two years. There might be days where I do a little better, but overall, since that injury disrupted my routine, it’s been pretty consistently causing problems.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of at least a few months where you didn't experience any of these difficulties with focus, restlessness, or impulsivity?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean… maybe there were stretches where it felt less noticeable because I had more of a routine, but I don’t remember a few months where it was just gone.\n\nThe main thing is, it’s been this disruptive for about a year and ten months now, basically ever since my soccer injury knocked me out of my usual routine. Before that, I could kind of get by better, or at least things were more held together because I had a structure. But I wouldn’t say the focus or restlessness or impulsive stuff disappeared for months. It was more like it was managed by the routine, and then once that got disrupted, it became way harder to control.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulties with focus and staying on task are more pronounced in quiet or low-stimulation environments?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. Quiet stuff is where it really shows up, especially if it’s something I’m supposed to just sit there and absorb.\n\nLike, I’ll try to watch a repair video for something, or read the manual for a tool, and I’ll genuinely mean to pay attention. I’m not trying to avoid it. But after a few minutes I’ll realize I’ve just been staring at the screen or looking at the page, and none of it actually went in. Then I have to back the video up, sometimes more than once, because I don’t remember what they just explained.\n\nIt’s frustrating because it feels like I was technically “doing” the task — I was sitting there, looking at it — but my brain wasn’t really staying with it. Low-stimulation stuff like that is definitely harder for me to stay locked onto.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulties with focus and staying on task are significantly impacting your daily life more than your restlessness and fidgeting?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I’d say the focus and staying-on-task part is the bigger problem day to day. The restlessness and fidgeting are annoying, and I know they’re there, but they don’t hold me back the same way.\n\nWhat’s really messing things up is that I can’t get through the steps I need to move forward. Like with trying to become an automotive technician — I know I can handle the hands-on work. When I’m actually doing something physical with cars or tools, that part makes sense to me. But the deadlines, the applications, the training requirements, all the paperwork-type steps… that’s where I keep getting stuck.\n\nI’ve missed training deadlines, and I’ve had to redo applications more than once because I either didn’t finish them right, didn’t submit something on time, or lost track of what part I was supposed to do next. And it’s happened enough now that I’m still not really moving forward, even though this is something I actually want to do and feel capable of doing.\n\nSo yeah, the fidgeting is there, but the focus and follow-through problems are what are actually affecting my life in a bigger way.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing.     "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, I mean… over the last couple years, the part I can say clearly is that it’s been really disruptive for about a year and ten months. That’s kind of the marker in my head, because it started getting a lot worse after my soccer injury knocked me out of my normal routine.

Before that, I had more structure around me, so I think I could sort of keep things contained better. But once that routine was gone, it started affecting me in a way that was much harder to ignore. So yes, in the sense that it hasn’t just been one bad week or one specific situation — it’s been this ongoing thing since then, across the stuff I’m trying to do day to day.

It’s not like I usually say anything rude, but inside I’m just very impatient and wanting it to be over with already.\n\nWith talking, I think I can wait sometimes, but if I have something I want to say, it’s hard not to jump in. I’ll be kind of waiting for the opening instead of fully relaxing into the conversation.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have difficulty keeping your workspace or living space organized?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. My room and my stuff can get disorganized pretty quickly, but I think the bigger issue is when there are several things I’m supposed to do and I don’t know how to sort them out in my head.\n\nLike if I have to call the counselor, clean up my room, and send in an application, those are all normal tasks, right? But instead of just picking one and finishing it, I’ll kind of stand there or sit there thinking, “Okay, what do I do first?” And then I’ll start cleaning for a few minutes, remember the application, open that up, then think I should make the phone call before it gets too late, but then I don’t have whatever information I need ready, so I go looking for that, and somehow I’ve touched all three things and none of them are actually done.\n\nSo it’s not always just that the space is messy, although it can be. It’s more like I don’t have a good system for where to start or how to keep going in order. I bounce around and half-finish things, and then the room or the desk ends up looking worse because I’ve pulled things out for multiple tasks at once.\n\nDoctor: Are these difficulties you're experiencing affecting your relationships, work, or daily life in a significant way?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it is affecting my daily life in a real way. The biggest example is with trying to move forward toward becoming an automotive technician.\n\nI know I can handle the hands-on part. Like, when I’m actually working with tools or dealing with something physical and practical, I feel like I can understand it and stay with it better. But the stuff around it — training deadlines, applications, paperwork, keeping track of what needs to be submitted and when — that’s where I keep messing up.\n\nI’ve missed training deadlines more than once, and I’ve had to redo applications because I either didn’t finish them right, didn’t get them in on time, or somehow let them sit too long until they weren’t usable anymore. And then it’s like I’m back at the beginning again. It’s frustrating because it’s not that I don’t want to do it. I do want to. I’m 26 and I feel like I should be moving forward by now, but I keep getting stuck on the steps that are supposed to get me there.\n\nSo, yeah, I’d say it’s significant. It’s not just annoying or inconvenient — it’s actually keeping me from getting into the work I think I could do.\n\nDoctor: Do you often experience strong emotions such as anxiety, anger, or excitement?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not totally sure how to answer that. I mean, I have emotions like anyone does, obviously, but I don’t know if I’d say I have, like, unusually strong anxiety or anger or excitement in a way that stands out to me.\n\nI can get frustrated sometimes, and I can get excited about things I care about, but I don’t know that I’ve really paid attention to whether it’s stronger than other people’s. It’s kind of hard to judge from the inside, you know? Like, if something irritates me, it feels normal in the moment because it’s my reaction.\n\nSo I guess… maybe sometimes, but I’m not sure. I wouldn’t want to say yes like it’s a major thing if I haven’t really noticed it clearly.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience these difficulties with focus, restlessness, and impulsivity at home, at work, or in social situations, or are they mainly in one of these areas?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure how to separate it out, honestly. Like, I notice it most at home, because that’s where I’m trying to manage my own stuff and there isn’t really someone standing over me telling me what the next step is. So at home it shows up a lot in trying to get things done, keep track of what I was doing, or not drift from one thing to another.\n\nWork is harder for me to answer because I’m not really in a regular job right now, so I don’t have as much to compare there. I can say that with work-related things, like applications or steps I’m supposed to complete, that’s where it becomes a problem. But I don’t know if that counts as “work” exactly or more like daily life.\n\nSocial situations… I don’t know. I haven’t really thought of it that way. I’m not saying it never happens around people, but it’s not the area where I’m most aware of it. I guess I’d say it’s not just one tiny situation, but I can explain it better with home and trying-to-get-things-done stuff than with social situations.\n\nDoctor: Have you always had these difficulties with focus and restlessness, or did they start at some point in your life?\n\nPatient: I think it’s been there for a long time, honestly. Like, when I think back, it doesn’t feel like something that suddenly started as an adult or after high school or anything. Even in elementary school, I remember teachers moving my seat around a lot because I’d talk out of turn or get distracted by whoever was near me.\n\nAnd homework was a big thing too — not always because I didn’t understand it, but because I’d forget it, or leave it somewhere, or not turn it in even if I had done part of it. I also remember rushing through worksheets just to get them done, and then there’d be these careless mistakes where, looking back, I probably could’ve gotten them right if I had slowed down.\n\nAt the time I don’t think I really understood it as a “focus” problem. It was more like I was always being told to sit somewhere else, stop talking, pay attention, or check my work. So yeah, I’d say it’s been around since I was a kid, not something that just came out of nowhere recently.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulties with staying on task and getting distracted are more of a problem for you than the restlessness and fidgeting?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. I mean, I do fidget and feel restless sometimes, but the bigger issue for me is definitely getting pulled off task.\n\nLike, I’ll sit down to do something that should be pretty straightforward, like working on an application, and then it’s like every little thing can knock me off course. A car goes by outside, my phone lights up, or I’ll suddenly think of some random thing I should check, and before I even really notice what happened I’m not doing the application anymore. I’m looking something up, or I’ve opened another tab, or I’m in another room doing some unrelated thing.\n\nIt’s not always even that I decide to stop. It’s more like my attention just gets grabbed by whatever is nearby or whatever thought pops up. Then later I realize, oh, I was supposed to be doing that other thing, and it’s frustrating because I didn’t mean to drift away from it. So yeah, compared to the fidgeting, the distraction and staying on task feels like the part that causes me more problems.\n\nDoctor: Did you have any of these difficulties with focus, restlessness, or impulsivity when you were a teenager?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. I mean, the stuff I remember most clearly is actually from even before being a teenager, like elementary school, so by the time I was a teenager it didn’t feel brand new or anything.\n\nI remember teachers always moving my seat because I’d talk out of turn or get distracted by whoever was near me. And homework was a recurring issue — I’d forget it, or not have it with me, or somehow not turn it in the way I was supposed to. I also used to rush through worksheets just to get them finished, and then I’d make these dumb careless mistakes that I probably would’ve caught if I had slowed down.\n\nSo, yeah, I’d say those kinds of problems were already there when I was younger and carried into that teenage period, rather than starting later.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your restlessness and fidgeting are as bothersome to you as your difficulty with staying focused and on task?\n\nPatient: Not as much, no. The focus thing bothers me more overall, because that’s the part where I feel like I actually lose track of what I’m supposed to be doing.\n\nThe restlessness and fidgeting is definitely there, though. Like if I’m sitting with someone, especially for more than a few minutes, my leg is usually bouncing under the table, or I’m clicking a pen, or I’ll be messing with my keys in my pocket or in my hand without even realizing I’m doing it. Sometimes I only notice because the other person looks at my hand, or I hear the clicking and realize, oh, that’s me doing that.\n\nIt can be embarrassing if I catch myself doing it, and I’ll try to stop, but then a minute later I’m doing something else with my hands or my foot starts moving again. So it’s annoying, and I’m aware of it, but I’d still say the staying-focused part causes me more trouble than the fidgeting itself.\n\nDoctor: Have your difficulties with focus, restlessness, and impulsivity caused problems in your relationships with friends or family?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think it has, although maybe not always in the obvious “we had a huge fight” kind of way. It’s more like people close to me can see that I’m not moving forward the way I keep saying I want to, and that creates tension.\n\nLike, I’ve been trying to move toward becoming an automotive technician, because I actually feel pretty confident with the hands-on part. And after it happens enough times, family especially starts asking, like, “Are you actually doing this?” or “Why haven’t you finished that yet?” And I get defensive, because I know it looks like I don’t care or I’m not trying, but that’s not really how it feels from the inside.\n\nSo yeah, it affects relationships because I think people get frustrated with me, and I get embarrassed and frustrated with myself. I don’t want to keep explaining why I’m still stuck in the same place when I know I could probably handle the actual work if I could just get through the steps to get there.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these difficulties with focus, restlessness, and impulsivity in multiple settings, such as at home, in social situations, and in different types of tasks?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I’d say it shows up in more than one place. I remember being moved closer to the teacher, or away from certain kids, or by myself, like the seating chart was always being changed because I couldn’t just sit there and keep quiet.\n\nAnd homework was a big thing too. I’d forget to bring it home, or I’d do it and forget to turn it in, or it would be crumpled up in my backpack somewhere. Then I have to back the video up, sometimes more than once, because I don’t remember what they just explained.\n\nIt’s frustrating because it feels like I was technically “doing” the task — I was sitting there, looking at it — but my brain wasn’t really staying with it. Low-stimulation stuff like that is definitely harder for me to stay locked onto.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulties with focus and staying on task are significantly impacting your daily life more than your restlessness and fidgeting?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I’d say the focus and staying-on-task part is the bigger problem day to day.
